  findings: The presence of an audience (even just the experimenter) increases the frequency and amount of third-party punishment of norm violators in both Trust Game and sequential Prisoner's Dilemma Game settings. Under anonymous conditions, punishment is infrequent and small; when observed, punishment is more common and larger. The effect is strongest when the audience includes other participants. The study does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs, and does not use a public goods game, but it demonstrates that reputational concerns strongly increase costly punishment behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it offers informative evidence that the salience of observation (audience effects) can substantially increase the willingness to engage in costly punishment. If a public goods game design includes features that make punishment decisions observable to others, one might expect higher rates of punishment, which could (in other contexts) affect cooperation and possibly efficiency. The lack of efficiency or payoff data limits direct applicability to the prediction task, but the findings are relevant for understanding moderators of punishment behavior.

- source: Goette, L., Huffman, D., Meier, S., & Sutter, M. (2012). Competition Between Organizational Groups: Its Impact on Altruistic and Antisocial Motivations. *Management Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that in a neutral environment, punishment is used prosocially to enforce cooperation, especially when the victim of defection is an in-group member. When competition between groups is introduced, punishment becomes antisocial: out-group members are punished more, regardless of whether they cooperate or defect. Competition increases in-group cooperation but does not reduce out-group cooperation. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the authors argue that antisocial punishment under competition is wasteful and could reduce overall efficiency, while increased in-group cooperation could enhance it. The net effect on efficiency is not measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of intergroup competition can fundamentally change the function of punishment in social dilemma games: from norm enforcement to antisocial, destructive punishment. While the design is not a standard repeated public goods game, the findings are highly relevant for predicting when punishment will be efficiency-enhancing versus efficiency-reducing. Specifically, in environments with salient group competition, enabling punishment may lead to resource-destroying antisocial punishment, undermining the efficiency gains typically associated with punishment. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data means the findings should be used as qualitative guidance rather than quantitative prediction.

- source: Li, J., Xiao, E., Houser, D., & Montague, P. R. (2009). Neural responses to sanction threats in two-party economic exchange. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a two-player repeated trust game, the threat of monetary sanctions (punishment) leads trustees to return significantly less to investors compared to when no sanction is threatened. This effect is robust across fair and unfair investor requests. Neural data show that the presence of a sanction threat reduces activation in brain regions associated with social reward (VMPFC, LOFC, amygdala) and increases activation in regions associated with rational, utility-based decision making (parietal cortex). The findings support the 'perception shift' hypothesis: sanction threats crowd out norm-based motivations and shift behavior toward self-interested, utility-maximizing choices. The study does not report group efficiency or total earnings, but the reduction in reciprocity implies a likely reduction in efficiency relative to the no-sanction condition.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a two-player repeated trust game, the introduction of a punishment (sanction) threat reduces cooperative behavior (reciprocity) by trustees. While the design is not a standard public goods game, the mechanism—punishment crowding out intrinsic motivation—may generalize to PGG-like settings. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment can reduce efficiency or group payoff in environments where cooperation is norm-driven and where punishment is perceived as a signal of distrust or as crowding out intrinsic motivation. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or total earnings, the effect on efficiency must be inferred from the reduction in reciprocity. The findings are most relevant for two-player, repeated, sequential games with optional punishment, and less directly for standard multi-player PGGs.

- source: Cameron, L., Chaudhuri, A., Erkal, N., & Gangadharan, L. (2009). Propensities to engage in and punish corrupt behavior: Experimental evidence from Australia, India, Indonesia and Singapore. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the propensity to punish corrupt behavior varies more across cultures than the propensity to engage in corruption. Indian subjects are most likely to offer and accept bribes and least likely to punish, while Indonesian subjects are least likely to engage in corruption and most likely to punish. The manipulation of the bribe's welfare impact (welfare-enhancing vs. welfare-reducing) only significantly affects behavior in Australia, where higher harm reduces punishment rates and increases bribery. In other countries, this manipulation has little effect. The main outcomes are behavioral (bribery and punishment rates/amounts), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on how punishment opportunities and the cost/harm structure of norm violations affect the willingness to punish and engage in norm-violating behavior in a corruption context. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The design is adjacent to PGGs: it is a three-player, one-shot, sequential-move game with endogenous costly punishment, but not a repeated or group-based public goods game. The main value for prediction is in understanding that punishment rates and their deterrent effect are highly culture-dependent and may not always translate into higher efficiency, especially when punishment is costly and the harm from norm violation is high. The manipulation of the harm from corruption (welfare-enhancing vs. welfare-reducing) shows that increasing the harm can sometimes reduce punishment and increase norm violation, due to the increased cost to the victim. This is relevant for predicting when punishment will be effective in improving efficiency, but the lack of direct efficiency data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Shinada, M., & Yamagishia, T. (2007). Punishing free riders: direct and indirect promotion of cooperation. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that third-party punishment is group-based: cooperators in the gift-giving game punish in-group cheaters more severely than out-group cheaters, while noncooperators show the opposite pattern. Most punishment is directed at the least cooperative group members. Emotional responses (anger, unfairness, guilt) are correlated with punishment of in-group cheaters but not out-group cheaters. The design uses a gift-giving game with a public-goods-like structure, but does not directly measure or report group efficiency or total payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the behavioral allocation of punishment in group-structured social exchange games, especially regarding in-group vs. out-group targeting. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games. The design and findings are most useful for understanding the social and motivational determinants of punishment, not its impact on group welfare or efficiency. The punishment mechanism (costly to punisher, 3x impact on target) and the absence of feedback or repeated interaction are relevant for mapping to other designs, but the lack of efficiency data limits direct predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Krasnow, M. M., Cosmides, L., Pedersen, E. J., & Tooby, J. (2012). What Are Punishment and Reputation for?. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiments show that (1) subjects use third-party reputation to regulate trust only when first-party (personal) information is unavailable; (2) punishment is not more likely to be directed at partners with a reputation for norm violation; (3) subjects are more likely to cooperate with defectors whom they have punished, consistent with punishment as a bargaining tool for future cooperation; (4) when deciding to cooperate, subjects rely on the partner's most recent behavior toward themselves, not on past reputation. The results support social exchange theory over group norm maintenance theory in the context of dyadic trust games with punishment. No direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about the psychological function of punishment in dyadic trust games, showing that punishment is used to bargain for better treatment rather than to enforce group norms. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game or a multi-player setting. The findings suggest that in small-scale, dyadic games, the presence of punishment may not increase group efficiency, but rather serves to regulate individual relationships. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in public goods games, this paper is only indirectly informative: it suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on whether the game context is dyadic or group-based, and on the psychological mechanisms engaged. The design dimensions most relevant here are the dyadic structure, the use of reputation, and the role of punishment as a bargaining tool.

- source: Henrich, J., McElreath, R., Barr, A., Ensminger, J., Barrett, C., Bolyanatz, A., Cardenas, J. C., Gurven, M., Gwako, E., Henrich, N., Lesorogol, C., Marlowe, F. W., Tracer, D., & Ziker, J. (2006). Costly punishment across human societies. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study demonstrates that costly punishment (both second-party and third-party) is a widespread human behavior, but its prevalence and intensity vary substantially across societies. In all populations, more unequal offers are punished more frequently, but the threshold for punishment and the willingness to punish differ. There is a strong positive correlation between a population's willingness to punish and its average altruistic behavior (as measured by dictator game offers). The findings support theories of gene-culture coevolution in human cooperation. However, the paper does not use public goods games and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so its direct relevance to predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments is limited.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that costly punishment is a robust and variable feature of human behavior across societies, and that it is positively associated with altruism. However, because the experiments are ultimatum and third-party punishment games (not public goods games), and because efficiency or group payoff is not reported, the findings are only indirectly informative for predicting the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The main implication is that the effect of punishment on cooperation-related behaviors is likely to be highly variable across populations, and that willingness to punish is linked to local norms of altruism. The paper does not provide direct evidence on how punishment affects efficiency or payoff in repeated or group-based public goods settings.

- source: Xiao, E., & Houser, D. (2005). Emotion expression in human punishment behavior. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that when responders in the ultimatum game are allowed to send a message to proposers expressing their feelings about the offer, the rejection rate of unfair offers (≤20% to responder) drops from 60% (no emotion expression allowed) to 32% (emotion expression allowed). This effect is statistically significant. The ability to express negative emotions through messages appears to reduce the use of costly punishment. The distribution of offers by proposers does not differ between treatments, indicating that the effect is due to responder behavior. The study does not report on efficiency or total group payoff, but the behavioral change would mechanically increase total earnings by reducing the frequency of zero-payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that, in environments where costly punishment is possible, introducing a non-costly channel for emotion expression can reduce the use of costly punishment. While the study is not a public goods game, it is highly relevant for understanding the psychological drivers of punishment and the potential for communication or emotion expression to moderate punishment behavior. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling communication or emotion expression may reduce the negative efficiency impact of punishment by reducing its frequency. However, the findings are specific to the ultimatum game and may not transfer directly to public goods games without further evidence.

- source: de Quervain, D. J. F., Fischbacher, U., Treyer, V., Schelthammer, M., Schnyder, U., Buck, A., & Fehr, E. (2004). The neural basis of altruistic punishment. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that when participants are given the opportunity to punish defectors in a trust game, they frequently do so, even at a personal cost. Effective (costly) punishment, as opposed to symbolic (non-impactful) punishment, is associated with increased activation in the dorsal striatum (caudate nucleus), a brain region implicated in reward processing. The amount of money spent on punishment correlates with the level of caudate activation, suggesting that anticipated satisfaction motivates punishment. The costliness of punishment reduces the amount of punishment compared to costless punishment, but does not eliminate it. The study does not report on group efficiency or total payoffs, but does show that punishment is used and is psychologically rewarding.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in trust-game-like environments, individuals are willing to pay to punish defectors, and that this behavior is motivated by anticipated satisfaction. However, the study does not report on group efficiency or total welfare, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a trust game with a punishment stage. The findings support the idea that costly punishment will be used when available, but do not provide direct evidence about whether this increases or decreases group efficiency. The costliness of punishment reduces its frequency/intensity, which may be relevant for predicting efficiency effects in similar designs.

- source: Marlowe, F. W., Berbesque, J. C., Barr, A., Barrett, C., Bolyanatz, A., Cardenas, J. C., Ensminger, J., Gurven, M., Gwako, E., Henrich, J., Henrich, N., Lesorogol, C., McElreath, R., & Tracer, D. (2008). More 'altruistic' punishment in larger societies. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the level of third-party punishment (as measured by MAO) is significantly higher in larger and more complex societies. Both local group size and ethnic group size are strong predictors of the willingness to punish unfair offers in a third-party punishment game. The results suggest that third-party punishment is more prevalent in larger societies, possibly as a response to increased anonymity and collective action problems. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the relationship between group size and the prevalence of third-party punishment in dictator-like games, which are adjacent to public goods games. It does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. However, it suggests that the willingness to punish norm violations increases with group size and social complexity, which may be relevant for understanding the conditions under which punishment is more likely to be used or effective. For the prediction task, this paper is mainly informative about the social and cultural moderators of punishment behavior, not about efficiency effects.

- source: Wang, C. S., Sivanathan, N., Narayanan, J., Ganegoda, D. B., Bauer, M., Bodenhausen, G. V., & Murnighan, K. (2011). Retribution and emotional regulation: The effects of time delay in angry economic interactions. *Organizational Behavior and Human Decision Processes*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across five lab experiments, the authors show that introducing a time delay before making punishment decisions (e.g., rejecting unfair offers or punishing norm violators) generally reduces costly punishment, but only if the delay is accompanied by distraction or cognitive reappraisal. If participants ruminate during the delay, punishment increases. The effect is mediated by negative emotions (anger). The experiments use one-shot or single-decision ultimatum games and third-party punishment trust games, with punishment measured as rejection rates or points spent. No direct group efficiency or total payoff outcomes are reported, but the reduction in costly punishment implies less wasteful destruction of resources. The findings highlight the importance of emotional regulation in moderating punitive impulses.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It shows that the context and psychological state of punishers (e.g., time delay, distraction, rumination) can strongly moderate the frequency and magnitude of costly punishment. While the games are not public goods games, the results suggest that interventions reducing negative emotion or promoting reappraisal/distraction could reduce inefficient punishment and thus improve group payoffs. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, and the games are not repeated or multi-player PGGs, the findings should be used as informative but indirect evidence for prediction tasks.

- source: Shinada, M., Yamagishi, T., & Ohmura, Y. (2004). False friends are worse than bitter enemies: Altruistic punishment of in-group members. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The bidirectional supervision mechanism, where players can check and avoid corrupt umpires, is effective at suppressing both corruption and defection in a spatial donation game with third-party punishment. The effectiveness of this mechanism depends on the punishment amount (must be within a certain range), the bribe amount (must not be too high), and the ratio of tax to checking cost (higher tax or lower checking cost is better). The benefit-to-cost ratio of cooperation has little effect within reasonable ranges. The main outcomes are the fractions of different strategies (cooperation, defection, corruption, fairness) in the population. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, but argues that the mechanism is most beneficial to collective interests when parameters are tuned appropriately.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that the design of punishment, bribe, tax, and checking cost parameters can strongly affect the prevalence of cooperation and corruption in a donation game with third-party punishment. However, because the main outcomes are behavioral (strategy fractions) and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as qualitative or mechanistic guidance rather than quantitative prediction of efficiency. The results suggest that punishment is only effective at suppressing defection and corruption if its severity is within a certain range and if corruption incentives (bribes) are not too high. The presence of a mechanism for players to avoid corrupt punishers is critical. The findings are most relevant for adjacent game designs with third-party punishment and corruption, but less directly transferable to standard public goods games or to quantitative efficiency prediction.

- source: Nelissen, R. M. A. (2008). The price you pay: cost-dependent reputation effects of altruistic punishment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The main finding is that individuals who invest more in costly third-party punishment are perceived as more fair and trustworthy, and are subsequently entrusted with more money in a trust game. This effect is contingent on the cost being a reliable signal (i.e., known relative to endowment). The effect is mediated by perceptions of fairness, not by general likability or generosity. The study does not measure group efficiency or public goods provision, but rather the reputational and trust consequences for punishers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on group efficiency in public goods games. However, it does provide indirect evidence that costly punishment can enhance the punisher's reputation and increase trust from others, but only when the cost is interpretable as a reliable signal. For prediction tasks focused on group efficiency in PGGs, this paper is only adjacent: it suggests that punishment may have reputational benefits for the punisher, which could, in theory, affect cooperation and efficiency in repeated or reputation-sensitive environments, but it does not test this directly. The design and outcomes are not directly transferable to PGG efficiency prediction.

- source: Perugini, M., Gallucci, M., Presaghi, F., & Ercolani, A. P. (2003). The personal norm of reciprocity. *European Journal of Personality*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that individual differences in positive and negative reciprocity (as measured by the PNR questionnaire) predict how people allocate money to others in response to fair or unfair prior actions, both in hypothetical and incentivized settings. Positive reciprocity predicts greater rewarding of positive behavior, while negative reciprocity predicts greater punishment of negative behavior. These effects are robust across cultures (Italy and UK) and are distinct from general prosocial orientation or Big Five personality traits. However, the studies do not directly examine group efficiency, total welfare, or the effect of punishment on group-level outcomes in repeated public goods or social dilemma games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that individual differences in the propensity to punish or reward (reciprocity norms) influence how people respond to others' actions in allocation games, including costly punishment and reward. However, it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on group efficiency or welfare in public goods games or similar repeated social dilemmas. The findings are most useful for understanding heterogeneity in punishment/reward behavior, not for predicting efficiency outcomes as a function of game design dimensions. The allocation tasks are adjacent to PGGs but lack repeated group interaction, explicit MPCR, or group efficiency measures.

- source: Pothos, E. M., Perry, G., Corr, P. J., Matthew, M. R., & Busemeyer, J. R. (2011). Understanding cooperation in the Prisoner's Dilemma game. *Personality and Individual Differences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that higher punishment severity, higher disguise cost, lower cost-to-benefit ratio, and larger group size all promote the extinction of defectors and the prevalence of cooperation (cooperators and punishers). When these parameters are less favorable, the system remains in a mixed state with both cooperators and defectors. The model shows that the system typically evolves to a state with only two strategies (either cooperators and punishers, or cooperators and defectors), and the boundaries between these regimes are mapped as functions of the key parameters. The results are robust to both deterministic replicator dynamics and stochastic mutation-selection dynamics in finite populations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect but informative evidence about how punishment parameters (cost, effectiveness), group size, and cost-to-benefit ratio affect the prevalence of cooperation in an N-person social dilemma with punishment. While the model is for the snowdrift game (not a standard PGG), and the main outcomes are behavioral frequencies rather than efficiency, the results suggest that increasing punishment severity, increasing group size, and increasing the cost for defectors to avoid punishment all make punishment more effective at promoting cooperation. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff ratios, so direct prediction of efficiency outcomes is not possible. The findings are most useful for understanding qualitative moderators of punishment effectiveness in related social dilemmas.

- source: Mathew, S., & Boyd, R. (2011). Punishment sustains large-scale cooperation in prestate warfare. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, show_punishment_id
  findings: The Turkana sustain costly, large-scale cooperation in warfare through informal punishment of free riders. Raiding parties are large and composed of unrelated individuals from multiple social units. Free riding (desertion, cowardice, unfair loot appropriation) is common, but is often sanctioned through verbal reprimands, corporal punishment, and fines. The system relies on third-party consensus and community enforcement, not formal institutions. Norms governing warfare are group-beneficial at the ethnolinguistic scale, not just for smaller social units. While the paper provides rich evidence on the mechanisms and scale of cooperation and punishment, it does not provide direct measures of efficiency or group payoff as defined in public goods games, nor does it experimentally manipulate punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative about the real-world operation of punishment and its role in sustaining cooperation at large scales, especially in the absence of formal institutions. However, it does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency or group payoff, nor does it use a controlled experimental design. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in public goods games, the paper offers strong qualitative support for the idea that punishment can sustain cooperation and reduce free riding, even in large, anonymous groups. The evidence is most relevant for understanding the mechanisms and social context of punishment, rather than for parameterizing efficiency outcomes in laboratory PGGs. The findings suggest that punishment can be effective at large group sizes and in the absence of repeated interaction or kinship, but do not provide direct evidence on efficiency changes due to punishment in a controlled game setting.

- source: Bingham, P. M. (1999). Human uniqueness: A general theory. *Quarterly Review of Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The central finding is that coalitional enforcement—cooperative, low-cost punishment of defectors—was necessary and sufficient for the evolution of large-scale, stable cooperation among unrelated individuals in humans. The theory predicts that as the efficiency and scale of coalitional enforcement increased (via technological and social monitoring advances), so did the extent and complexity of human cooperation. The paper discusses how this mechanism underlies the emergence of language, culture, large political units, and rapid technological progress. However, the paper does not provide direct evidence or quantitative findings about efficiency or payoffs in public goods game-like environments; rather, it offers a theoretical framework for understanding the evolutionary roots of cooperation and punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that punishment (specifically, coalitional or group-based punishment) can enable and stabilize high levels of cooperation among non-kin, by reducing the individual cost of enforcement and making defection unprofitable. For the downstream prediction task, the paper suggests that the presence and efficiency of punishment mechanisms are critical moderators of group cooperation and, by extension, group efficiency. However, because the paper does not report empirical or experimental data on efficiency or payoffs in public goods games, it cannot be used to calibrate quantitative predictions. Its main value is in supporting the general principle that more efficient, lower-cost, and more widely distributed punishment mechanisms should be associated with higher group cooperation and efficiency, all else equal.

- source: Balafoutas, L., & Nikiforakis, N. (2012). Norm enforcement in the city: A natural field experiment. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that some individuals are willing to engage in costly punishment to enforce social norms in one-shot, anonymous field interactions, but this is rare (11.7% overall, 4% for non-littering, 19.3% for escalator norm). Men are more likely to punish than women; the violator's height and gender do not significantly affect enforcement rates. The main reason for not punishing is fear of retaliation. The better-known (universal) norm (non-littering) is enforced less often than the environment-specific norm (escalator), despite being more widely valued. The study does not measure or report group efficiency, payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on the willingness to engage in costly punishment in real-world, one-shot norm violation settings, but does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its findings suggest that, in natural field settings, the rate of costly punishment is much lower than in laboratory public goods games, possibly due to higher perceived costs (e.g., risk of retaliation) and lack of anonymity. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in public goods games, this paper suggests that the mere availability of punishment may not translate into high rates of enforcement or efficiency gains in field-like conditions. However, because no group payoff or efficiency data are reported, the paper cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes from design dimensions or control efficiency. Its main value is in highlighting the behavioral limits of punishment in naturalistic settings and the importance of context (e.g., fear of counter-punishment, norm universality) as moderators.

- source: Boehm, C. (2008). Purposive Social Selection and the Evolution of Human Altruism. *Cross-cultural Research*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, reward_exists, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that in small-scale, mobile hunter-gatherer societies, both punitive and positive social sanctioning are pervasive and target behaviors relevant to group cooperation, such as sharing, generosity, and suppression of dominance or cheating. Punishment (including ostracism and even capital punishment) is used to control antisocial behaviors, while positive sanctioning (reputation, social approval, preferential partnerships) rewards prosocial behaviors. The author theorizes that these mechanisms have had significant evolutionary effects, shaping both the prevalence of altruism and the development of conscience in humans. However, the findings are based on ethnographic coding and theoretical reasoning, not on experimental or quantitative analysis of efficiency or payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and ethnographic support for the idea that punishment and reward mechanisms are central to the maintenance of cooperation in small-scale societies, and that they have long-term evolutionary consequences. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the plausibility of punishment as a mechanism for sustaining cooperation, but does not provide data or effect sizes relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes as a function of game design dimensions.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
