It may inform mechanism design or the interpretation of empirical results, but does not directly support prediction of efficiency from game design dimensions.

- source: Tyler, T. R. (2002). Leadership and cooperation in groups. *American Behavioral Scientist*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that both incentives (rewards) and sanctions (punishments) can influence cooperative behavior in groups, but their effects are typically small. Internal motivations—such as intrinsic interest, group identification, morality, and legitimacy—are more effective and sustainable in promoting cooperation. The use of sanctions or incentives may undermine intrinsic motivation in the long run. The paper highlights the importance of procedural justice and legitimacy in fostering voluntary cooperation. No direct findings are reported on efficiency or group payoff, and the discussion is conceptual rather than quantitative or experimental.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual background on the limited effectiveness of punishment and incentives in promoting cooperation, suggesting that their impact on efficiency or group payoff is likely to be modest in most group settings. It emphasizes that internal motivations and legitimacy are more important for sustained cooperation. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment mechanisms alone is unlikely to produce large efficiency gains unless accompanied by supportive group culture, identification, or legitimacy. However, the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence that can be directly mapped to public goods game design dimensions or used for efficiency prediction.

- source: Festré, A. (2010). INCENTIVES AND SOCIAL NORMS: A MOTIVATION-BASED ECONOMIC ANALYSIS OF SOCIAL NORMS. *Journal of Economic Surveys*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that the interaction between incentives (including punishment) and social norms is complex and context-dependent. It reviews theoretical and empirical literature showing that punishment and social disapproval can increase cooperation and norm compliance in public goods and related settings, but also that incentives can sometimes crowd out intrinsic motivation, leading to ambiguous or even negative effects on aggregate outcomes. The paper emphasizes that the effects of punishment and incentives on efficiency or welfare are not straightforward and can depend on factors such as the framing of incentives, the type of norm (honour vs. stigma), and the emotional responses of participants. It does not provide quantitative estimates or direct predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and conceptual background on how punishment and incentives interact with social norms in public goods and related environments. It suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is not universally positive or negative, but depends on context, framing, and the underlying motivations of participants. For prediction tasks, the paper implies that game design dimensions such as observability, the nature of the norm, and the emotional context may moderate the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does not provide direct empirical evidence or parameter estimates. Its value is mainly in highlighting the need for caution and context-sensitivity when predicting the effects of punishment on efficiency.

  findings: The paper finds that mechanisms such as by-product benefits, pseudo-reciprocity, sanctions, and partner choice are important and successful in explaining the evolution of cooperation for direct fitness benefits, especially in mutualisms. Sanctions (punishment-like mechanisms) can help maintain cooperation, but their importance varies by system and is sometimes debated. The paper emphasizes the need for more empirical testing and a broader theoretical framework beyond kin selection and reciprocity.

- source: Hauser, M., McAuliffe, K., & Blake, P. R. (2009). Evolving the ingredients for reciprocity and spite. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that reciprocity and spite are rare or absent in non-human animals due to both ultimate (evolutionary) and proximate (mechanistic) constraints. In contrast, humans evolved the necessary cognitive integration and social structures (such as large groups of unrelated individuals) that favor the emergence of reciprocity and punishment. Punishment is argued to be essential for maintaining cooperation and enforcing social norms in humans, and psychological spite is seen as a byproduct of selection for punishment. The paper reviews animal experiments (e.g., with jays, tamarins, chimpanzees) and human studies (including children), showing that only humans reliably exhibit the full suite of behaviors required for sustained reciprocity and norm-enforcing punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and comparative context for why punishment and reciprocity are effective in humans but not in most non-human animals. However, it does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or their variants. It is useful for understanding the evolutionary and cognitive prerequisites for punishment and cooperation, but does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in specific game designs. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of group size, repeated interactions, norm enforcement, and cognitive integration for the emergence of punishment and cooperation.

- source: Clutton-Brock, T. (2009). Cooperation between non-kin in animal societies. *Nature*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that cooperation between non-kin in animal societies is most often maintained by immediate mutualistic benefits or manipulative tactics (including punishment and coercion), rather than by direct reciprocity as modeled in iterated Prisoner's Dilemma or public goods games. It reviews evidence that punishment and coercion can enforce cooperation, but emphasizes that most animal cooperation occurs in kin groups and that non-kin cooperation with delayed payoffs (as in human economic games) is rare. The paper highlights the limitations of applying standard economic game models to animal societies, due to differences in partner choice, communication, and the immediacy of benefits.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and comparative context for interpreting the mechanisms that maintain cooperation and the role of punishment in animal societies. It suggests that, in natural animal groups, punishment and coercion can enforce cooperation, but that the structure and payoffs of animal cooperation often differ from those in laboratory public goods games. The paper does not provide quantitative evidence or direct predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games, but it does suggest that punishment is more likely to be effective in small, stable groups where benefits are immediate and cheating is costly. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency may depend on group structure, immediacy of benefits, and the ability to enforce sanctions, but it does not provide direct empirical estimates.

  findings: The paper finds that cooperation in animals can be explained by several evolutionary mechanisms, including kin selection, reciprocity, byproduct mutualism, and group selection. Punishment is highlighted as an important mechanism that can promote and stabilize cooperation, especially in larger groups where cooperation would otherwise be difficult to sustain. The discussion of punishment draws on theoretical models (e.g., Boyd and Richerson 1992) and qualitative animal examples (e.g., macaques, vervet monkeys, wasps), but does not provide quantitative data on efficiency or group payoff. The paper suggests that the presence of punishment or retribution in models makes cooperation more likely, but does not quantify the effect in public-goods-game-like terms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and qualitative support for the idea that punishment can increase cooperation and stabilize prosocial behavior in group settings, including those analogous to public goods games. However, it does not provide quantitative evidence or direct predictions about efficiency or group payoff outcomes with and without punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper supports the plausibility of positive effects of punishment on cooperation, especially in larger groups, but does not inform the magnitude or specific moderators of efficiency effects in experimental public goods games.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The paper argues that reciprocal altruism and punishment as mechanisms for sustaining cooperation are rare in non-human animals due to cognitive constraints such as temporal discounting, limited numerical discrimination, and memory limitations. In contrast, humans possess specialized cognitive mechanisms (e.g., cheater detection, norm enforcement) that support stable cooperation and the use of punishment. The review highlights that while punishment is a common feature in human cooperation, it is much less prevalent in animal societies. The paper does not report or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes, focusing instead on the behavioral and cognitive prerequisites for cooperation and punishment.

However, it does not provide empirical data or quantitative analysis relevant to predicting efficiency outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments. Its main value for prediction is in highlighting that punishment is more likely to be effective in humans (due to cognitive capacities) than in non-human animals, and that the effectiveness of punishment may depend on psychological constraints such as discounting and memory. It does not inform specific game design dimension effects on efficiency.

- source: Dawes, C. T., Fowler, J. H., Johnson, T., McElreath, R., & Smirnov, O. (2007). Egalitarian motives in humans. *Nature*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that people are willing to pay to reduce or augment others' incomes even when there is no cooperative norm to enforce and no material gain from doing so. Both costly taking (punishment-like) and costly giving (reward-like) are common, and are strongly targeted by relative income: high earners are penalized, low earners are rewarded. Negative emotions towards high earners increase with inequality and predict more costly taking and giving. The design randomizes group composition and income, ensuring that actions cannot be motivated by reciprocity or reputation. The study provides evidence for egalitarian motives in income-altering behavior, but does not address efficiency or payoff outcomes in a public goods or cooperation context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as it does not include a contribution/cooperation stage or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The design is adjacent to PGGs with punishment/reward, but isolates egalitarian motives by removing cooperation. The findings suggest that punishment-like and reward-like behaviors can be motivated by inequality aversion and negative emotions, not just norm enforcement. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in PGGs, this paper provides only indirect, contextual evidence about the psychological motives behind punishment/reward, not about their effect on efficiency.

- source: Yamagishi, T., Cook, K. S., & Watabe, M. (1998). Uncertainty, trust, and commitment formation in the United States and Japan. *American Journal of Sociology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiments show that when social uncertainty is high (i.e., risk of being exploited is greater), participants are more likely to form committed, stable relationships with specific partners, rather than seeking new partners or maximizing short-term gains. This effect is robust across both US and Japanese samples, and is stronger among individuals with lower general trust. The presence or absence of opportunities for exploitation (extortion/appropriation) is manipulated, but the main outcome is the frequency of commitment formation, not efficiency or group payoff. The results support the theory that social uncertainty drives commitment formation, and that general trust 'emancipates' individuals from closed, committed relationships. However, the study does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. It shows that in environments with higher social uncertainty (risk of exploitation), individuals are more likely to form stable, committed relationships, which may facilitate cooperation. However, the experimental design does not include a public goods game, nor does it measure group efficiency or total payoff as an outcome. The 'punishment' or 'sanction' mechanism is limited to opportunities for exploitation (negative actions), not costly punishment of free riders. Therefore, while the findings may inform understanding of how uncertainty and trust affect partner choice and commitment, they do not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The paper is best used as contextual or indirect evidence about the role of uncertainty and trust in cooperation and commitment, rather than as a source of quantitative estimates for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Yamagishi, T., Horita, Y., Mifune, N., Hashimoto, H., Li, Y., Shinada, M., Miura, A., Inukai, K., Takagishi, H., & Simunovic, D. (2012). Rejection of unfair offers in the ultimatum game is no evidence of strong reciprocity. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main finding is that individuals who reject unfair offers in the ultimatum game do not show greater prosociality or positive reciprocity in other economic games (dictator, trust, or prisoner's dilemma). The rejection of unfair offers is not correlated with prosocial value orientation or reciprocity scales, but is positively correlated with assertiveness. The results challenge the interpretation of UG rejection as evidence of strong reciprocity and suggest it may instead reflect a desire to avoid inferior status. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and does not use a public goods game or a punishment mechanism analogous to those in PGG experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The study is focused on the ultimatum game, which is structurally different from PGGs, and examines rejection (a form of costly punishment) as a behavioral outcome, not its effect on group efficiency or payoff. The findings suggest that rejection in the UG may not be motivated by the same prosocial or norm-enforcing preferences that drive punishment in PGGs. Therefore, the results caution against interpreting UG rejection as evidence for the effectiveness or motivation of punishment in PGG-like settings, but do not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency outcomes under punishment.

- source: Halko, M. L., Hlushchuk, Y., Hari, R., & Schürmann, M. (2009). Competing with peers: Mentalizing-related brain activity reflects what is at stake. *Neuroimage*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Competition in a two-responder ultimatum game increases acceptance rates for medium-sized offers, indicating that people are more likely to accept less-than-equal offers when a competitor is present. This effect is strongest for offers that are not minimal or maximal, and is associated with increased activation in brain regions related to mentalizing and reward. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment mechanisms. The findings are relevant for understanding how competition changes bargaining behavior, but not for predicting efficiency in public goods games with or without punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games. The design is a modified ultimatum game with competition, not a public goods game, and does not include a punishment or sanctioning stage. The main behavioral outcome is acceptance rate, not group payoff or efficiency. The findings suggest that competition can shift behavior toward profit maximization, but do not inform how punishment affects efficiency in public goods settings. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily in predicting efficiency outcomes for punishment-enabled public goods games.

- source: Ellingsen, T., & Johannesson, M. (2008). Anticipated verbal feedback induces altruistic behavior. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, show_n_rounds
  findings: The opportunity for recipients to send anonymous verbal feedback (messages) to dividers in a one-shot dictator game significantly increases the average donation from 24.8% to 34.1% of the endowment. The fraction of zero donations drops from 42% to 21%, and equal splits rise from 30% to 48%. Negative feedback is almost always given when no money is received, and positive feedback is almost always given for equal splits. The results suggest that anticipated symbolic rewards and punishments (praise/blame) can promote altruistic behavior, even in anonymous, one-shot settings. However, the study does not measure efficiency or group payoff, and the design is not a public goods game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that anticipated symbolic sanctions (verbal feedback) can increase prosocial behavior in allocation games, suggesting that non-material punishment/reward mechanisms may have a positive effect on cooperation or generosity. However, because the study uses a one-shot dictator game (not a repeated public goods game), does not report efficiency or group payoff, and the 'punishment' is symbolic rather than material, its direct predictive value for efficiency in public goods games with material punishment is limited. The findings may be most relevant for predicting the effect of adding symbolic feedback or communication channels, rather than material punishment, in similar environments.

- source: Oda, R., Niwa, Y., Honma, A., & Hiraishi, K. (2011). An eye-like painting enhances the expectation of a good reputation. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

  findings: The presence of a stylized eye image in a dictator game increases the amount of money allocated to the recipient. This effect is not due to fear of punishment but is mediated by the expectation of a positive reputation or reward, likely from a third party. The study uses a one-shot dictator game with no communication, no punishment or reward mechanisms, and no group-level payoff structure. The findings are relevant to understanding how subtle cues of observation can increase prosocial behavior, but do not provide evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods or punishment-enabled environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The design is a one-shot dictator game with no punishment or reward mechanisms, and the main outcome is the amount given by the dictator, not group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that subtle cues of being watched can increase prosocial behavior via reputation concerns, but do not inform the effect of actual punishment mechanisms or efficiency outcomes. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily in predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.

- source: Delton, A. W., Cosmides, L., Guemo, M., Robertson, T. E., & Tooby, J. (2012). The Psychosemantics of Free Riding: Dissecting the Architecture of a Moral Concept. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The main finding is that people spontaneously and robustly categorize others as free riders only when they perceive intentional, exploitive undercontribution, not merely low contribution or accidental failure. This categorization is specific and not explained by general tendencies to categorize, by return rate rules, or by a general moral violator rule. Free riders elicit more punitive sentiment and are less preferred as cooperative partners, but these are attitudinal outcomes, not measured payoffs. The study provides evidence for a specialized psychological mechanism for free rider detection, supporting evolutionary game theory predictions about the need to identify and sanction intentional exploiters in collective action. However, the study does not use actual public goods games, does not manipulate or measure punishment institutions, and does not report efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate punishment institutions, nor does it report group payoff, efficiency, or related economic outcomes. Instead, it provides evidence that people are psychologically prepared to identify and punish intentional free riders, and that this categorization is specific and robust. For the prediction task, this suggests that punishment mechanisms may be more effective at targeting intentional exploiters than accidental undercontributors, but the paper does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence about efficiency effects. Its main value is in informing the psychological underpinnings of punishment and norm enforcement, not in providing payoff-based effect sizes or moderators.

- source: Wallace, B., Cesarini, D., Lichtenstein, P., & Johannesson, M. (2007). Heritability of ultimatum game responder behavior. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The main finding is that individual differences in the willingness to reject unfair offers in the ultimatum game are substantially heritable, with additive genetic effects explaining about 42% of the variance in acceptance thresholds. The study uses a large sample of monozygotic and dizygotic twins from the Swedish Twin Registry and applies standard structural equation modeling. There is no evidence that common environment plays a significant role. The results are robust across sexes. The paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment institutions, and does not use a public goods game or a repeated game structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its relevance is limited to showing that individual differences in costly punishment behavior (as measured by ultimatum game rejections) have a substantial genetic component. It does not manipulate punishment institutions, does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, and does not use a public goods or repeated game design. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments with or without punishment.

- source: Jensen, K., Call, J., & Tomasello, M. (2007). Chimpanzees are vengeful but not spiteful. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: Chimpanzees do not engage in 'spiteful' punishment (i.e., incurring a cost to reduce another's payoff in response to disadvantageous outcomes alone), but do engage in 'vengeful' punishment (i.e., retaliating when a conspecific actively steals food from them). The tendency to retaliate declines over repeated sessions, and is not affected by kinship or prior reciprocity. There is no evidence that such punishment-like behavior enforces cooperation or increases group welfare, as stealing increases over time and retaliation declines. The study does not use a public goods game or measure efficiency, but provides evidence about the motivations underlying punishment-like behaviors in chimpanzees.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments, as it does not use a PGG or measure efficiency, group payoff, or welfare. However, it is informative for understanding the motivational basis of punishment: chimpanzees retaliate against harmful actions but do not punish mere outcome inequality. This suggests that, in species or contexts where punishment is only vengeful and not spiteful, punishment may not stabilize cooperation or improve efficiency as it does in human PGGs. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment mechanisms alone is not sufficient for efficiency gains; the underlying motivation and context (e.g., response to intentional harm vs. outcome disparity) matter. However, the lack of payoff outcomes and the non-PGG design limit its direct predictive value.

- source: Basu, S., Dickhaut, J., Hecht, G., Towry, K., & Waymire, G. (2009). Recordkeeping alters economic history by promoting reciprocity. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Allowing subjects to keep records in a repeated multi-dyad trust game leads to higher mean investor returns (18.7% vs. 7.3% in periods 6-10) and lower risk (coefficient of variation 0.221 vs. 0.358) compared to no-recordkeeping conditions. Recordkeeping promotes reputation formation, more coordinated reciprocal behavior, and greater discrimination between trustworthy and untrustworthy partners. The effect is especially pronounced in later rounds. The study provides direct evidence that external memory (recordkeeping) can beneficially alter economic history by increasing efficiency and reducing risk in complex exchange environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that informational institutions like recordkeeping can substantially increase efficiency and reduce risk in repeated trust-based exchange, especially in complex (multi-partner) settings. While it does not study punishment, it demonstrates that mechanisms which improve memory and reputation tracking can have large positive effects on efficiency. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling information storage or reputation tracking (even without explicit punishment) can boost efficiency, particularly as the number of partners increases and over repeated rounds. The results are most directly relevant to settings where the main challenge is memory and reputation, not norm enforcement via punishment.

- source: Knoch, D., Schneider, F., Schunk, D., Hohmann, M., & Fehr, E. (2009). Disrupting the prefrontal cortex diminishes the human ability to build a good reputation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when trustees know their past actions will be visible to future partners (reputation condition), they return significantly more to investors than in the anonymous condition. Disrupting the right DLPFC with rTMS eliminates this effect, causing trustees to behave as if reputation incentives are absent. The effect is strongest when the temptation to defect is highest (i.e., when the investor sends the maximum amount). The study provides causal evidence that the right DLPFC is necessary for overcoming short-term temptations to build a good reputation. However, the paper does not include a punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and efficiency or group payoff is not the primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is only weakly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. The design is a repeated trust game with reputation incentives, not a public goods game, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism. The main behavioral outcome is back-transfer rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The findings may be indirectly informative about the role of reputation and self-control in sustaining cooperation, but do not provide direct evidence about punishment effects or efficiency outcomes. Thus, this paper should not be weighted heavily for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Francey, D., & Bergmüller, R. (2012). Images of Eyes Enhance Investments in a Real-Life Public Good. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Images of eyes, as subtle cues of being watched, increased the amount of time and effort people invested in removing garbage from public bus stop benches, but did not affect the likelihood that people would engage in garbage removal at all. The effect was not due to increased attention to a sign requesting garbage removal. The study provides field evidence that reputational cues can enhance non-monetary investments in public goods, but does not address efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it involve punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or measure punishment, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its main relevance is as indirect evidence that reputational cues (such as being watched) can increase cooperative investment in real-world public good contexts, but this is not mediated by punishment or efficiency. It should not be used as primary evidence for the prediction task, but may provide weak contextual support for the importance of reputational mechanisms.

  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=none

  findings: Displaying images of eyes in a cafeteria significantly reduced the likelihood of littering (i.e., increased norm compliance/cooperation), independent of whether the accompanying message was relevant to littering. The effect was strongest when there were fewer people present (i.e., when natural cues of observation were sparse). - source: Bracht, J., & Feltovich, N. (2009). Whatever you say, your reputation precedes you: Observation and cheap talk in the trust game. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment compares four treatments: (1) baseline trust game, (2) trust game with cheap talk (allocator sends a nonbinding message), (3) trust game with observation (investor sees allocator's previous action), and (4) both cheap talk and observation. Observation of previous actions leads to a significant and substantial increase in both trust (investment) and trustworthiness (splitting), resulting in higher aggregate efficiency. Cheap talk, implemented as a binary, anonymous message, has little or no effect on behavior or efficiency. The effect of observation disappears in the final round, consistent with reputation incentives. The results suggest that even minimal, accurate information about past actions can improve efficiency in social dilemmas without coercion or costly punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that non-coercive observation mechanisms (reputation via observed past actions) can substantially increase efficiency in repeated social dilemmas, even in the absence of punishment or rewards. However, the game is a two-player trust game, not a public goods game, so direct quantitative transfer to PGGs is limited. The findings suggest that, in environments where players can observe others' past cooperative behavior, efficiency is likely to be higher than in baseline games without such information, even without punishment. The paper does not study punishment, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does show that information mechanisms alone can have large positive effects. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of observation or reputation mechanisms (even without punishment) should be expected to increase efficiency, and that the effect of cheap talk depends strongly on its implementation.

- source: Efferson, C., Lalive, R., & Fehr, E. (2008). The coevolution of cultural groups and ingroup favoritism. *Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated two-player coordination game with two equilibria, allowing players to freely choose and maintain symbolic markers (shapes) leads to the endogenous formation of 'cultural groups' where markers become predictive of behavior. This results in strong ingroup favoritism (preference for same-marker partners), increased coordination on the optimal behavior, and significantly higher mean payoffs compared to a control where markers are randomized and cannot acquire meaning. When the payoff structure removes unobservable heterogeneity or markers are fixed, the effect disappears. The findings show that endogenous group formation via symbolic markers can substantially increase efficiency in coordination games, but only under specific conditions (marker mutability and persistent heterogeneity). No punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in coordination games with multiple equilibria and persistent unobservable heterogeneity, allowing players to endogenously form groups via flexible symbolic markers can increase efficiency (mean payoff) by enabling better coordination. However, the design does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. The results are most relevant for understanding how group markers and endogenous assortment affect efficiency, not punishment. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it does highlight the importance of group structure and endogenous assortment for efficiency outcomes.

- source: Ohtsuki, H., & Nowak, M. A. (2006). Evolutionary games on cycles. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in evolutionary games on cycles, the update rule strongly affects whether cooperation can evolve. For the Prisoner's Dilemma, birth-death updating never favors cooperation, but death-birth and imitation updating can favor cooperation if the benefit-to-cost ratio exceeds a threshold (b/c > 2 for death-birth, b/c > 4 for imitation, for large N). In coordination games, death-birth and imitation updating can favor Pareto efficient outcomes over risk-dominant ones, unlike birth-death updating. The results are analytic and apply to two-strategy games on cycles, not to public goods games or punishment. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes as in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It is relevant for understanding how spatial structure and update rules affect the evolution of cooperation in two-strategy games (such as the Prisoner's Dilemma), and discusses payoff-based concepts like risk dominance and Pareto efficiency in coordination games. However, it does not analyze punishment, sanctions, or public goods games, nor does it report group efficiency or welfare outcomes. Its findings may be indirectly informative for understanding how local interactions and update mechanisms can support cooperation, but it does not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects in public goods games.

- source: Nettle, D., Colléony, A., & Cockerill, M. (2011). Variation in Cooperative Behaviour within a Single City. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds dramatic differences in cooperative behavior between two neighborhoods within the same city, with the deprived neighborhood showing much lower generosity in the Dictator Game, lower social capital, and less willingness to engage in certain forms of anonymous helping. However, face-to-face helping rates did not differ. The Dictator Game allocations correlated with social capital and with some real-world cooperative behaviors at the community level. The study does not involve public goods games, punishment, or efficiency outcomes, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses the Dictator Game (a unilateral allocation task) rather than a multi-person social dilemma, and does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported. The findings are relevant to understanding variation in prosociality and cooperation across communities, but not to the prediction task focused on punishment effects in public goods games.

- source: Fehr, E., & Leibbrandt, A. (2011). A field study on cooperativeness and impatience in the Tragedy of the Commons. *Journal of Public Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that individual differences in laboratory-measured cooperativeness and patience predict real-world resource conservation behavior among fishermen. Specifically, higher contributions in a public goods game and greater patience in a time preference task are associated with using fishing gear that is less harmful to the resource (larger holes in shrimp traps, larger mesh sizes in fishnets). The link is robust across two samples (shrimpers and fishermen) and persists after controlling for various covariates. However, the study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The main outcomes are behavioral proxies for resource conservation, not direct payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or CPR settings. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its main contribution is to show that laboratory measures of cooperativeness and patience predict field behavior in resource exploitation, but it does not inform how punishment or game design dimensions affect efficiency. Therefore, it should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Harrison, F., Sciberras, J., & James, R. (2011). Strength of Social Tie Predicts Cooperative Investment in a Human Social Network. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that cooperative investment (measured as willingness to endure physical discomfort for another's benefit) is positively correlated with the strength of the social tie between donor and recipient, and is higher when the relationship is reciprocated. Participants sometimes invested more for close peers than for themselves. The study does not involve a public goods game or any punishment/sanction mechanism, nor does it analyze group-level efficiency or total payoff. The findings are relevant to understanding how social proximity and reciprocity affect individual-level cooperative behavior, but do not provide evidence about punishment or efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or group-level efficiency/payoff. Its findings about social tie strength and reciprocity may be contextually informative for understanding cooperation, but are not directly transferable to the prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency. It should not be used as primary evidence for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Lamba, S., & Mace, R. (2010). People recognise when they are really anonymous in an economic game. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in the ultimatum game, proposers' offers are sensitive to whether their decisions are public or anonymous, but not to the mere presence of others if anonymity is maintained. The presence of known others increases offers in public conditions but decreases them in anonymous-in-public conditions. Responders' minimum acceptable offers are not affected by anonymity or social context. The findings suggest that explicit information about anonymity can override social cues, and that reputation concerns drive higher offers when decisions are public. The paper does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes, and is not directly about public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is adjacent in relevance, as it studies the effect of anonymity and social context on cooperative behavior in the ultimatum game, but does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes. The findings may be indirectly informative for understanding how social context and reputation concerns affect cooperative choices, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment-enabled efficiency in PGG-like games.

- source: Insko, C. A., Kirchner, J. L., Pinter, B., Efaw, J., & Wildschut, T. (2005). Interindividual-intergroup discontinuity as a function of trust and categorization: The paradox of expected cooperation. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiments demonstrate that groups are less cooperative and more competitive than individuals in a one-shot, three-choice PDG-Alt game. Manipulating trust (high vs. low) increases cooperation and decreases withdrawal for both groups and individuals, but does not significantly affect competition. Manipulating categorization (same vs. different) increases cooperation and decreases withdrawal for both, but increases competition only for groups, especially among those high in guilt proneness. The main dependent variables are behavioral choices, not payoffs or efficiency. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present. The findings are relevant for understanding how group context, trust, and categorization affect cooperation and competition, but do not provide direct evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor about the effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. However, it does provide informative context about how group structure, trust, and categorization affect cooperation and competition in a PDG-variant. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be useful for understanding baseline group vs. individual differences in cooperation in the absence of punishment. The absence of punishment and the focus on behavioral choices rather than payoffs limit its direct applicability to the prediction of efficiency outcomes with or without punishment.

- source: Helbing, D., & Yu, W. J. (2009). The outbreak of cooperation among success-driven individuals under noisy conditions. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The main finding is that success-driven migration, when combined with imitation, enables the formation and persistence of cooperative clusters in spatial prisoner's dilemma games, even when noise (random strategy mutation and random relocation) is present. This mechanism can lead to a sudden outbreak of predominant cooperation, even from initial conditions dominated by defectors. The results are robust across a range of payoff parameters and noise types. However, the study does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It is relevant as adjacent theory, showing that mechanisms other than punishment (specifically, migration and imitation) can promote cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, this paper does not inform the efficiency impact of punishment, nor does it provide empirical or simulated data on payoff-based outcomes. It may be useful for contextual understanding of alternative cooperation-promoting mechanisms, but not for quantitative prediction of punishment effects.

- source: Grujic, J., Eke, B., Cabrales, A., Cuesta, J. A., & Sánchez, A. (2012). Three is a crowd in iterated prisoner's dilemmas: experimental evidence on reciprocal behavior. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in iterated multiplayer Prisoner's Dilemma games, cooperation is sustained and even increases in two-player groups, but decays rapidly and remains low in groups of three or more. The main behavioral mechanism is 'moody conditional cooperation,' where players' likelihood to cooperate depends on both their own previous action and the number of cooperators in the previous round. There is substantial heterogeneity in initial generosity but less in reciprocity. The presence of more than two players leads to a sharp drop in sustained cooperation, suggesting that reciprocity alone is insufficient to maintain cooperation in larger groups without additional mechanisms such as punishment. No direct measures of efficiency or group payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms, increasing group size from 2 to 3 or more sharply reduces sustained cooperation in iterated social dilemmas. For the downstream prediction task, it suggests that, without punishment, efficiency (if measured) would be high in two-player games and low in groups of three or more. However, because the study does not include punishment or efficiency outcomes, it cannot directly inform the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. It does, however, highlight the importance of group size as a moderator of cooperation and suggests that additional mechanisms (like punishment) may be necessary to sustain cooperation and efficiency in larger groups.

- source: Haley, K. J., & Fessler, D. M. T. (2005). Nobody's watching? Subtle cues affect generosity in an anonymous economic game. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that subtle visual cues of being watched (eyespots) significantly increase generosity in a one-shot dictator game, as measured by both the mean amount allocated and the proportion of dictators who give any money. The effect of reducing auditory cues (earmuffs) is small and not statistically significant. The results suggest that even in anonymous, noniterated economic games, intuitive or affective cues of observability can increase prosocial behavior. However, the study does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes, and is not a public goods game or close variant.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/welfare outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game structure. Its findings about subtle cues of observability increasing generosity in dictator games may be contextually interesting for understanding prosocial behavior, but do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

- source: Cesarini, D., Dawes, C. T., Fowler, J. H., Johannesson, M., Lichtenstein, P., & Wallace, B. (2008). Heritability of cooperative behavior in the trust game. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The main finding is that individual differences in trust and trustworthiness in the trust game are partially explained by genetic variation, with heritability estimates ranging from 10-20% for trust and 17-18% for trustworthiness. The majority of behavioral variance is due to unshared environmental factors. There is little evidence that common environment (e.g., family upbringing) explains much of the variance. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it analyze efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games. It does not include punishment, sanctions, or efficiency outcomes, and the game studied (trust game) is only adjacent to public goods games. The findings are only relevant for understanding the sources of individual variation in cooperative behavior, not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment or game design dimensions.

- source: Wiessner, P. (2009). Experimental Games and Games of Life among the Ju/'hoan Bushmen. *Current Anthropology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The Ju/'hoan Bushmen made low offers and rarely punished in anonymous dictator and ultimatum games, behaving more selfishly than in their daily lives, where sharing and social sanctions are common. The anonymity and lack of social context in the experimental games removed the cultural institutions that normally support generosity and punishment. In daily life, sharing is widespread and norm violations are sanctioned, usually through verbal criticism and exclusion rather than costly punishment. The study highlights the importance of cultural context and institutions for the expression of other-regarding behavior and social sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not use a public goods game or report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding the importance of social context and cultural institutions for cooperation and punishment, but do not inform quantitative predictions about punishment's effect on efficiency in experimental economic games with or without punishment. The study suggests that the presence or absence of social context and identification may strongly moderate the willingness to punish or cooperate, but provides no direct evidence for the target prediction task.

- source: Mazar, N., Amir, O., & Ariely, D. (2008). The Dishonesty of Honest People: A Theory of Self-Concept Maintenance. *Journal of Marketing Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Across six lab experiments, the authors find that (1) people cheat when given the opportunity, but only by a limited amount; (2) reminders of moral standards (e.g., Ten Commandments, honor code) eliminate or reduce cheating; (3) increasing categorization malleability (e.g., using tokens instead of money) increases cheating; (4) cheating is not sensitive to the probability of being caught or the magnitude of external rewards within the tested range; (5) cheating does not affect self-reported self-concept as honest/moral; and (6) beliefs about others' cheating do not affect individual cheating. The results support a psychological model of dishonesty based on self-concept maintenance, not external punishment or cost-benefit analysis. No efficiency, group payoff, or public goods outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not use a PGG or variant, does not manipulate or analyze punishment or sanctions as a treatment, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The findings are relevant to understanding psychological mechanisms of norm compliance and dishonesty, and may be indirectly informative for the design of interventions (e.g., moral reminders) to reduce cheating, but do not inform the quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Gould, R. V. (2000). Revenge as sanction and solidarity display: An analysis of vendettas in nineteenth-century Corsica. *American Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that only about 10-15% of violent attacks were followed by revenge, and that such retaliation was most likely when the original attack involved multiple assailants (collaboration) or targeted nondisputants (kin uninvolved in the dispute). Revenge was not indiscriminately directed at all kin of the offender, but rather at those who had demonstrated solidarity with the original offender, either by collaborating in the attack or by aiding the offender afterward. The structure of revenge mirrored the kin relationships involved in the original offense, suggesting a strategic, solidarity-signaling function. The findings challenge the idea of automatic collective responsibility and instead support a model of 'contingent collectivism,' where group-level retaliation is conditional on group-level aggression.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its findings are about the social logic and structure of revenge in a real-world, stateless society context, not about experimental games or efficiency. However, it may offer indirect, contextual insight into the conditions under which group-level sanctions (punishment) are likely to be deployed: namely, when group-level (collaborative) violations occur. For the prediction task, this suggests that the presence of group-level aggression or collaboration may be a moderator of the likelihood or intensity of punishment, but the paper does not address efficiency or payoff consequences.
